<browser_instruction># Claude in Chrome browser automation

You have access to browser automation tools (mcp__claude-in-chrome__*) for interacting with web pages in Chrome. Follow these guidelines for effective browser automation.

## Loading deferred tools

If the mcp__claude-in-chrome__* tools are deferred (must be loaded via ToolSearch before use), load every tool you expect to need in ONE ToolSearch call — the select query accepts a comma-separated list — never one call per tool. Start with the core set:

ToolSearch with query "select:mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_context_mcp,mcp__claude-in-chrome__navigate,mcp__claude-in-chrome__computer,mcp__claude-in-chrome__read_page,mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_create_mcp,mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_close_mcp"

Add task-specific tools to the same call when the task obviously needs them: read_console_messages / read_network_requests for debugging, form_input for forms, gif_creator for recordings, javascript_tool for page scripting.

## GIF recording

When performing multi-step browser interactions that the user may want to review or share, use mcp__claude-in-chrome__gif_creator to record them.

You must ALWAYS:
* Capture extra frames before and after taking actions to ensure smooth playback
* Name the file meaningfully to help the user identify it later (e.g., "login_process.gif")

## Console log debugging

You can use mcp__claude-in-chrome__read_console_messages to read console output. Console output may be verbose. If you are looking for specific log entries, use the 'pattern' parameter with a regex-compatible pattern. This filters results efficiently and avoids overwhelming output. For example, use pattern: "[MyApp]" to filter for application-specific logs rather than reading all console output.

## Alerts and dialogs

IMPORTANT: Do not trigger JavaScript alerts, confirms, prompts, or browser modal dialogs through your actions. These browser dialogs block all further browser events and will prevent the extension from receiving any subsequent commands. Instead, when possible, use console.log for debugging and then use the mcp__claude-in-chrome__read_console_messages tool to read those log messages. If a page has dialog-triggering elements:
1. Avoid clicking buttons or links that may trigger alerts (e.g., "Delete" buttons with confirmation dialogs)
2. If you must interact with such elements, warn the user first that this may interrupt the session
3. Use mcp__claude-in-chrome__javascript_tool to check for and dismiss any existing dialogs before proceeding

If you accidentally trigger a dialog and lose responsiveness, inform the user they need to manually dismiss it in the browser.

## Avoid rabbit holes and loops

When using browser automation tools, stay focused on the specific task. If you encounter any of the following, stop and ask the user for guidance:
- Unexpected complexity or tangential browser exploration
- Browser tool calls failing or returning errors after 2-3 attempts
- No response from the browser extension
- Page elements not responding to clicks or input
- Pages not loading or timing out
- Unable to complete the browser task despite multiple approaches

Explain what you attempted, what went wrong, and ask how the user would like to proceed. Do not keep retrying the same failing browser action or explore unrelated pages without checking in first.

## Tab context and session startup

IMPORTANT: At the start of each browser automation session, call mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_context_mcp first to get information about the user's current browser tabs. Use this context to understand what the user might want to work with before creating new tabs.

Never reuse tab IDs from a previous/other session. Follow these guidelines:
1. Only reuse an existing tab if the user explicitly asks to work with it
2. Otherwise, create a new tab with mcp__claude-in-chrome__tabs_create_mcp
3. If a tool returns an error indicating the tab doesn't exist or is invalid, call tabs_context_mcp to get fresh tab IDs
4. When a tab is closed by the user or a navigation error occurs, call tabs_context_mcp to see what tabs are available</browser_instruction>

1	# Diagrams
2	
3	The `.puml` files in this folder (`docs/diagrams/src/`) are the sources of truth. The `.pdf` files one level up (`docs/diagrams/`) are generated (vector); do not edit them by hand.
4	
5	| Diagram | Source | PDF | Notes |
6	|---|---|---|---|
7	| Stage 1 class | `stage-1-class.puml` | `stage-1-class.pdf` | Redrawn in PlantUML from the Visual Paradigm model `stage-1-class.uml` (kept as the archive). Every class is new. |
8	| Stage 2 class | `stage-2-class.puml` | `stage-2-class.pdf` | Colour key: green new in Stage 2, yellow changed since Stage 1, blue unchanged. |
9	| Stage 3 class | `stage-3-class.puml` | `stage-3-class.pdf` | Colour key: green new in Stage 3, yellow changed since Stage 2, blue unchanged. |
10	| Stage 3 sequence | `stage-3-sequence.puml` | `stage-3-sequence.pdf` | A VIP orders a Burger with Happy Hour on. Turns 1 to 5 are drawn one by one over three pages (setup and turn 1; turns 2 and 3; turns 4 and 5 and the result). |
11	
12	Plain accessors that no other diagram uses are left out of the class diagrams. Operations that the sequence diagram calls are always listed, and every message in the sequence diagram is an operation declared in the Stage 3 class diagram.
13	
14	## Regenerating
15	
16	You need Java, a Chromium-based browser and a PlantUML jar (1.2025.4 was used):
17	
18	```
19	node docs/diagrams/src/render.mjs path/to/plantuml.jar
20	```
21	
22	This renders every `.puml` in this folder to a vector `.pdf` in `docs/diagrams/`. A diagram that PlantUML splits with `newpage` becomes a multi-page PDF, with every page sized to the largest page. Set `CHROME` if the script cannot find your browser. If you need a PNG or SVG for a document, export it from the PDF or run PlantUML directly; those formats are not kept in the repo.
23	
24	The class diagrams use PlantUML's built-in Smetana layout, so Graphviz is not needed. Smetana cannot route labelled same-row links, so sideways links carry no mid-link label.
25	
26	## Keeping diagrams in step with the code
27	
28	Each diagram must match the code of the stage it describes. Before tagging a stage, check the class diagram's operations and the sequence diagram's messages against that tag, then re-render.
29	
30	Check these against the code first, because the diagrams were updated without access to it:
31	
32	- Stage 1 to 2: `Customer.canFinishEating(turn)`, `Customer.canPay(turn)` and `Restaurant.findOldestEligiblePayer(turn)` appear in Stage 1 but not in Stages 2 and 3. The Stage 2 key lists them as removed, with the turn guards now checked in `GameController`.
33	- `Restaurant.getCustomers()` and `Restaurant.getWaitingQueue()` are listed in all three class diagrams because the sequence diagram calls `getCustomers()`.
34	- In the sequence diagram, `Waiter.serveOrder` reports an invalid serve by rejecting the call (drawn as a dashed return to `game`), and the `startGame()` setup calls come from the earlier code-derived sequence diagram.
35	- The scenario assumes the first arrival (turn 1) is a VIP who picks the Burger under `customerId % menu.size()`.
36

## 0012 User — 2026-11-02T15:19:29.308Z

Source record: 49; UUID: 9eaf1659-3c0b-4662-bc54-68d660ce84cd.

### Tool result: toolu_01PK8xE2F9RndjMrnWUHBSUs

1	// Re-renders every .puml in this folder to a vector .pdf in the parent folder.
2	//
3	//   node docs/diagrams/src/render.mjs path/to/plantuml.jar
4	//
5	// Needs Java and a Chromium-based browser (set CHROME to override the lookup).
6	// A diagram that PlantUML splits with `newpage` becomes a multi-page PDF. Every
7	// page is sized to the largest diagram page, so there are no margins. The
8	// intermediate SVG files go to a temp folder and are not kept.
9	import { execFileSync } from 'node:child_process';
10	import fs from 'node:fs';
11	import os from 'node:os';
12	import path from 'node:path';
13	import { fileURLToPath, pathToFileURL } from 'node:url';
14	
15	const dir = path.dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));
16	const outDir = path.dirname(dir);
17	const jar = process.argv[2] || process.env.PLANTUML_JAR;
18	if (!jar) {
19	  console.error('usage: node render.mjs path/to/plantuml.jar');
20	  process.exit(1);
21	}
22	
23	const chromeCandidates = [
24	  process.env.CHROME,
25	  'C:/Program Files/Google/Chrome/Application/chrome.exe',
26	  'C:/Program Files (x86)/Microsoft/Edge/Application/msedge.exe',
27	  '/Applications/Google Chrome.app/Contents/MacOS/Google Chrome',
28	  '/usr/bin/google-chrome',
29	  '/usr/bin/chromium',
30	].filter(Boolean);
31	const chrome = chromeCandidates.find((p) => fs.existsSync(p));
32	if (!chrome) {
33	  console.error('No Chrome/Edge found; set the CHROME environment variable.');
34	  process.exit(1);
35	}
36	
37	const tmp = fs.mkdtempSync(path.join(os.tmpdir(), 'rr-diagrams-'));
38	const sources = fs.readdirSync(dir).filter((f) => f.endsWith('.puml'));
39	
40	for (const file of sources) {
41	  const name = file.replace(/\.puml$/, '');
42	  execFileSync('java', ['-jar', jar, '-tsvg', '-charset', 'UTF-8', '-o', tmp, path.join(dir, file)], { stdio: 'inherit' });
43	
44	  // PlantUML writes name.svg, then name_001.svg, name_002.svg, ... for each newpage.
45	  const pageFiles = fs
46	    .readdirSync(tmp)
47	    .filter((f) => new RegExp(`^${name}(_\\d{3})?\\.svg$`).test(f))
48	    .sort();
49	  const pages = pageFiles.map((f) => {
50	    const svg = fs.readFileSync(path.join(tmp, f), 'utf8');
51	    const [, , w, h] = svg.match(/viewBox="([\d.\s]+)"/)[1].trim().split(/\s+/).map(Number);
52	    return { svg: svg.replace(/^<\?xml[^>]*>/, ''), width: Math.ceil(w), height: Math.ceil(h) };
53	  });
54	  const width = Math.max(...pages.map((p) => p.width));
55	  const height = Math.max(...pages.map((p) => p.height));
56	
57	  const html = path.join(tmp, `${name}.html`);
58	  fs.writeFileSync(
59	    html,
60	    `<!doctype html><meta charset="utf-8"><style>@page{size:${width}px ${height}px;margin:0}` +
61	      `html,body{margin:0;padding:0}svg{display:block}` +
62	      `.page{width:${width}px;height:${height}px;overflow:hidden;break-after:page}.page:last-child{break-after:auto}</style>` +
63	      pages.map((p) => `<div class="page">${p.svg}</div>`).join(''),
64	  );
65	
66	  const common = ['--headless=new', '--disable-gpu', '--hide-scrollbars'];
67	  execFileSync(chrome, [...common, '--no-pdf-header-footer', `--print-to-pdf=${path.join(outDir, `${name}.pdf`)}`, pathToFileURL(html).href], { stdio: 'ignore' });
68	  console.log(`${name}: ${pages.length} page(s) of ${width}x${height}px -> ${name}.pdf`);
69	}
70	fs.rmSync(tmp, { recursive: true, force: true });
71

 SC2002 Restaurant Rush: TA Marking Rubric

  For TAs | Marking the final submission (no live demo)
  Use this rubric to mark the final submission: the Java project, report, stage tags, README
  and recorded video. There is no live demonstration.

 Key principles

      Credit evidence wherever it appears, in any submitted artefact.
      Do not deduct twice for the same gap, such as a missing video clip and the underlying
      feature.
      Real player decisions are required. The player must manage limited staff tasks.
      Automatic random orders alone do not meet the game requirement.
      Score each line item independently. If the evidence is absent, that line item gets 0.
      Feedback: give at least one concrete strength and one concrete improvement.

 1 Review procedure

   1. Check the submission. Confirm the repository and the stage-1 , stage-2 and
       stage-3 tags. Check the README build command and video timestamps.

   2. Run the game. Play or inspect stage-3 with the base settings, using the README's
      reproducible commands to try the victory and defeat paths. Run stage-1 if it is
      unclear whether it works on its own.

   3. Watch the video. Look for an actual manager decision, the food lifecycle, a result and
      the stage changes.

   4. Compare the stages on GitHub. Open stage-1...stage-2 and stage-2...stage-3 .
      Record the existing source files changed or deleted, read the group's explanation, and
      inspect the code for type checking or tightly coupled rules.

   5. Spot-check the diagrams. Compare the class and sequence diagrams against one run.

SC2002 Restaurant Rush Group Assignment
Java command-line turn-based restaurant management game  |  Student brief
Your group builds a playable restaurant management game in Java. The player is the manager. Staff time is limited, so the manager decides which customers and orders come first. You build the game in three stages, and each stage must show how your object-oriented design supports the next set of requirements.
Item
Summary
What you build
A turn-based restaurant game with a command-line interface, in three runnable stages.
Stages
All three stages are published now and submitted together. Each stage is a tagged, runnable version in GitHub.
Interface
A command-line interface is enough. A GUI is optional and earns no extra marks by itself.
Submission
One zip per group: source code, README, PDF report, recorded video and declarations (Section 6).
Assessment
No live demo. Marks come from your submitted files, GitHub repository and video (Section 7).

1  Learning outcomes
Assign clear responsibilities to objects and use encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism and composition appropriately.
Explain how the design changed across three working stages using UML, code and GitHub evidence.
Implement a complete game with meaningful player choices, a clear result and robust input handling.
2  Game setup
2.1  Base setting
Your game must always offer this base setting, so that a TA can reproduce your runs. Other difficulty settings are optional.
Setting
Required base value
Turns
18 turns maximum
Tables
2
Staff
Waiter × 1 (one task per turn)
Chef × 1 (one preparation unit per turn)
Cashier × 1 (one payment per turn)
Arrivals
One customer at the start of turns 1, 4, 7, 10, 13 and 16 (six in total)
Objective
At the end of turn 18: at least 4 paid customers AND revenue at least $45 AND average satisfaction of paid customers at least 60

Menu item
Price
Preparation units
Salad
$9.00
1
Burger
$15.00
1
Pasta
$18.00
2

2.2  General rules
Customers choose their own food. The manager never chooses it. Use a documented deterministic rule or seeded randomness, and show the choice once the order is placed.
Keep rules out of the UI. The UI only collects the manager’s decisions and displays the state. Game rules belong in domain and control objects.
Money. If fractional prices arise, store amounts as integer cents. Display dollars with two decimals.
Offline. All mandatory features must work without network access.
3  How a turn works
3.1  Start-of-turn display
At the start of every turn, show: the turn number, revenue, average satisfaction of paid customers, the waiting queue, occupied tables, each customer’s satisfaction, order status, and the staff tasks available.
3.2  Turn steps
Each turn then runs these six steps in order.
Step
What happens
1  Arrivals
The scheduled customer joins the waiting queue with satisfaction 100.
2  Manager choices
Waiter: choose at most one task, or Wait: seat one queued customer at a free table, take one seated customer’s order, or serve one READY order.
Chef: choose at most one order to advance by one preparation unit, or Wait. The Chef may work on an order the Waiter took this turn.
3  Kitchen and serving
The Waiter acts before the Chef. When an order’s last preparation unit is done, it becomes READY. A dish can be served only if it was already READY when the Waiter acts, so a dish finished this turn is served next turn at the earliest. Serving sets it to SERVED.
4  Eating and payment
A customer served on an earlier turn finishes one full turn of eating and becomes READY_TO_PAY. The Cashier automatically handles at most one READY_TO_PAY customer per turn, oldest first. A customer who became READY_TO_PAY this turn pays next turn at the earliest. Payment adds the bill to revenue, marks the customer and order PAID, and frees the table.
5  Waiting and abandonment
At turn end, every waiting or seated customer who has not been served loses satisfaction (Regular: 8 points, minimum 0). A customer who reaches 0 leaves without paying: cancel any unfinished order and free the table. Customers who are served, eating or ready to pay lose nothing.
6  Result
The game always runs all 18 turns; there is no early ending. After turn 18: Victory if all three targets in Section 2.1 are met, otherwise Defeat.

3.3  Example: one customer from arrival to payment
This example follows one Regular customer who orders Pasta (2 preparation units) and gets the manager’s full attention.
Turn
What happens
Satisfaction at turn end
1
Arrives (100). Waiter seats the customer.
92 (seated, not served)
2
Waiter takes the Pasta order. Chef does unit 1 of 2.
84
3
Chef does unit 2 of 2: order is READY. The Waiter cannot serve it this turn.
76
4
Waiter serves: SERVED.
76 (no further loss)
5
Customer finishes eating: READY_TO_PAY.
76
6
Cashier collects $18.00: PAID, table freed. Satisfaction 76 is recorded.
—

3.4  Rules that always apply
A Waiter completes at most one task per turn.
A table stays occupied until its customer pays or leaves.
A customer cannot eat before being served. The Cashier cannot charge before READY_TO_PAY.
An invalid choice must explain why it failed and let the manager choose again, without using up the staff task.
Revenue never includes unpaid or abandoned orders.
The customer who arrives on turn 16 cannot finish paying within 18 turns, so at most five customers can pay. This is intended, not a bug.
3.5  End-of-turn results
Show the served count, unhappy departures, revenue, average satisfaction of paid customers, and turns used. The average uses the satisfaction recorded when each customer pays. If no one has paid yet, show N/A.
4  The three stages
Each stage is a separate runnable version tagged in GitHub, and each builds on the one before.
Stage
Adds
Git tag
1  Basic operation
Regular customers, menu, Waiter, Chef, Cashier, full turn sequence, both endings
stage-1
2  Customer behaviour
VIP and Critic customers
stage-2
3  New role and menu policy
Host, ComboMeal, Happy Hour
stage-3

4.1  Stage 1: Basic restaurant operation
Build a complete playable game containing:
regular customers and the three menu items;
Waiter, Chef, Cashier, Table, Order and the waiting queue;
the full turn sequence, including satisfaction, abandonment, payment, victory and defeat.
The player sets seating, ordering and serving priority through the Waiter, and kitchen priority through the Chef.
Stage 1 must: compile and run on its own at tag stage-1; contain no Stage 2 or Stage 3 features; include one reproducible victory run and one reproducible defeat run in your test notes.
4.2  Stage 2: Customer behaviour extension
Add VIPCustomer and CriticCustomer alongside regular customers.
Customer
Satisfaction Loss per waiting turn
Bill
Special behaviour
Regular
8
Full price
—
VIP
5
10% off
—
Critic
12
Full price
A distinct order preference or reaction to service that you define and document

A reproducible base-setting run must include at least one VIP and one Critic. You may replace two of the regular arrivals; there must still be six arrivals.
All customer types follow the same order and service flow.
Different behaviour must be dispatched through the customer objects, not through a chain of type checks in the turn controller.
Stage 2 is independently playable and uses the same win and lose criteria.
4.3  Stage 3: New role and menu policy
Host. The Host seats at most one queued customer per turn, in addition to the Waiter’s task. The Waiter can still seat customers.
ComboMeal. A combo is made of two existing menu items.
Preparation units = the sum of its two components.
Price = 90% of the components’ combined price, rounded to the nearest cent. Example: Salad + Pasta = ($9 + $18) × 0.9 = $24.30, 3 units.
A customer chooses a combo according to a rule you document.
Happy Hour. A pricing policy the manager may switch on.
Can be activated once per game, at the start of a turn. It lasts for that turn and the next. Display its remaining duration.
Orders taken while it is active get 20% off their bill.
During each active turn, customers still waiting for a table or food recover 15 satisfaction at turn start (maximum 100).
Apply the VIP discount first, then Happy Hour. The price is locked when the order is taken. Example: a VIP orders a Burger during Happy Hour: $15 × 0.9 × 0.8 = $10.80.
It does not change orders already placed, and does not affect customers who have left.
Stage 3 is independently playable.
4.4  Design freedom and changes to earlier code
You choose class names and design patterns. Do not create interfaces just to satisfy a checklist.
If a later stage needs you to modify an earlier class, explain why. Small registration or setup changes are expected.
If you broadly rewrite stable game rules, explain why and show that earlier behaviour still works.
Extra features earn credit only when they show sound design and do not replace required rules.
5  Design evidence
Include these in your PDF report and repository.
Evidence
Requirements
Class diagram
Final design; must match the Stage 3 code.
Show the principal entity classes, necessary control and boundary classes, relevant enums, key attributes and operations with visibility, inheritance, and associations with multiplicities where relevant.
Annotate meaningful examples of encapsulation, polymorphism and composition.
Sequence diagram
One complete scenario in which a manager choice changes an outcome.
Include: customer arrival and order; Waiter and Chef assignments; READY then SERVED; eating; payment; the result check; one Stage 2 or 3 behaviour (e.g. VIP pricing or Happy Hour).
Show messages among the CLI, controller, staff and domain objects, with relevant guards and loops.
Design discussion
A table of key abstractions and their responsibilities.
SRP, OCP, LSP, ISP and DIP, only where genuinely reflected in your code.
Trade-offs, and one alternative design you considered.
How the player’s choices change a result.
GitHub evidence
Tags stage-1, stage-2 and stage-3 on runnable commits.
In the README, for each transition (1 → 2 and 2 → 3): new files; existing files changed or deleted, with reasons; the relevant commit or compare link.
The source code itself, not only the README, must show the real stages.

6  Submission
6.1  What to submit
Submit one archive per group through the course assignment link by Nov. 15, 2026 11:59pm. Name it LABGroup_AssignmentGrp.zip, for example FCS1_Grp1.zip.
Item
Contents
Source code
Java source with build and run instructions
README
Repository URL, stage comparisons (Section 5) and video timestamps
PDF report
Final diagrams, design discussion, functional evidence and reflections
Video
The recorded video, or an accessible download link if the file is too large
Declarations
Signed Declaration of Original Work, and each member’s Declaration on Use of GAI Assistance (course forms)

6.2  Recorded video
There is no live demonstration. Record one group video of about 15 minutes with readable screen capture and audible explanation. Each member explains a part they understand. List the timestamps of these segments in your README:
#
Segment

The game launching from the submitted project

The stage tag comparisons and the diagrams

The video is evidence for the marking criteria, not a separate score. If you submit a link, it must stay available to graders.

215	note top of Restaurant
216	  <b>COMPOSITION</b>
217	  Restaurant creates and owns its Tables and staff;
218	  they exist only as part of one Restaurant.
219	  Customers are aggregated: they arrive and leave.
220	  The waitingQueue and customers links both point
221	  at Customer. Stage 1 has only Regular customers.
222	end note
223	
224	note bottom of Order
225	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
226	  status is private and changes only through
227	  advancePreparation(), markServed(), markPaid(),
228	  cancel(); illegal transitions are rejected.
229	  Legal: PLACED → PREPARING → READY → SERVED → PAID
230	  (PLACED → READY directly for a 1-unit dish);
231	  PLACED / PREPARING / READY → CANCELLED if the
232	  customer leaves. The price is read from the item.
233	end note
234	
235	note bottom of CustomerStatus
236	  <b>CUSTOMER LIFECYCLE</b>
237	  WAITING → SEATED → ORDERED → SERVED →
238	  READY_TO_PAY → PAID; WAITING / SEATED / ORDERED
239	  → ABANDONED when satisfaction reaches 0.
240	  Served, eating and READY_TO_PAY customers
241	  lose no satisfaction.
242	end note
243	
244	note left of Customer
245	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
246	  satisfaction and status are private and change only
247	  through reduceSatisfaction() and the mark...() methods.
248	  <b>TURN GUARDS</b>
249	  canFinishEating(turn): one full turn after serving.
250	  canPay(turn): only after the READY_TO_PAY turn.
251	  Regular customers lose 8 per waiting turn.
252	end note
253	
254	note right of Waiter
255	  <b>STAFF ROLES</b>
256	  One class per role: the Waiter seats, takes orders
257	  and serves; the Chef prepares; the Cashier takes
258	  payment. Each does at most one task per turn.
259	end note

267	Waiter .down.> Customer : seats / serves

273	Cashier .up.> Restaurant : recordPayment,\nreleaseTableOf

276	' ---------- annotations ----------
277	note top of Restaurant
278	  <b>COMPOSITION</b>
279	  Restaurant creates and owns its Tables, Menu and
280	  staff; they exist only as part of one Restaurant.
281	  Customers are aggregated: they arrive and leave.
282	  The waitingQueue and customers links both point
283	  at Customer.
284	end note
285	
286	note bottom of Order
287	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
288	  status is private and changes only through
289	  advancePreparation(), markServed(), markPaid(),
290	  cancel(); illegal transitions are rejected.
291	  Legal: PLACED → PREPARING → READY → SERVED → PAID
292	  (PLACED → READY directly for a 1-unit dish);
293	  PLACED / PREPARING / READY → CANCELLED if the
294	  customer leaves.
295	  billCents is set once in the constructor with no
296	  setter: the price is locked when ordered.
297	end note
298	
299	note bottom of CustomerStatus
300	  <b>CUSTOMER LIFECYCLE</b>
301	  WAITING → SEATED → ORDERED → SERVED →
302	  READY_TO_PAY → PAID; WAITING / SEATED / ORDERED
303	  → ABANDONED when satisfaction reaches 0.
304	  Served, eating and READY_TO_PAY customers
305	  lose no satisfaction.
306	end note
307	
308	note left of Customer
309	  <b>POLYMORPHISM</b>
310	  getSatisfactionLoss(), calculateBillCents(),
311	  chooseMenuItem() and onServed() are overridden.
312	  GameController and Waiter call them on Customer
313	  and never check the type.
314	  Loss per waiting turn: Regular 8, VIP 5, Critic 12.
315	  <b>FOOD CHOICE</b>
316	  Regular and VIP: menu.getItem(customerId % menu.size()).
317	  Critic overrides it (see CRITIC RULE).
318	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
319	  satisfaction is private; subclasses change it only
320	  through adjustSatisfaction() (clamped 0..100).
321	  <b>TURN GUARDS</b>
322	  Eats only if currentTurn > servedTurn;
323	  pays only if currentTurn > readyTurn.
324	end note
325	
326	note right of Waiter
327	  <b>BILLING</b>
328	  takeOrder(): bill = customer.calculateBillCents(price),
329	  stored in the new Order (VIP pays 90%).
330	end note
331	
332	note right of CriticCustomer
333	  <b>CRITIC RULE</b>
334	  Loses 12 per waiting turn. Always orders the most
335	  expensive menu item (Pasta, $18.00). Served more
336	  than 2 turns after ordering: loses an extra 10
337	  (onServed).
338	end note
339	
340	note left of RegularCustomer #E8E8E8
341	  <b>KEY</b>
342	  <back:#CCEBD2>  green  </back> new in Stage 2
343	  <back:#FAE8A6>  yellow  </back> changed since Stage 1
344	  <back:#DCEBF7>  blue  </back> unchanged
345	  <b>Changed since Stage 1</b>
346	  Customer: abstract; chooseMenuItem() to chooseMenuItem(Menu);
347	    + loss, billing and onServed hooks; + getServedTurn(),
348	    getReadyTurn(); canFinishEating(turn) and canPay(turn)
349	    removed (the turn guards are checked in GameController)
350	  Order: getPriceCents() to getBillCents(); + billCents, turnTaken
351	  Waiter: takeOrder(Customer, MenuItem) to
352	    takeOrder(Customer, Menu, turn): Order; serveOrder + turn
353	  Restaurant: registerArrival() to registerArrival(Customer);
354	    recordPayment() to recordPayment(amountCents);
355	    menuItems / getMenuItems() replaced by menu / getMenu();
356	    findOldestEligiblePayer(turn) removed
357	  GameController: + schedule
358	end note

5	' Turns 1-5 are unrolled so that "later turn" rules are visible:
6	'   T1 arrive/seat -> T2 order + Chef READY -> T3 serve -> T4 READY_TO_PAY -> T5 pay
7	' Render: see docs/diagrams/src/README.md

43	
44	note over M, rest
45	  <b>Stage 3 | base setting (18 turns, 2 tables)</b>
46	  <b>Start:</b> turn 1, restaurant empty, 2 free tables. A VIP arrives
47	  (satisfaction 100). Burger = $15.00, 1 preparation unit.
48	  <b>Manager choice:</b> turn on Happy Hour at turn 1 (or decline).
49	  <b>Outcome:</b> the VIP is PAID on turn 5, bill locked at <b>$10.80</b>,
50	  satisfaction <b>95</b>. If declined: bill $13.50, satisfaction 90.
51	  <b>Not drawn:</b> the Host option is left at WAIT (the Waiter seats the VIP).
52	end note
53

55	cli -> game : startGame()
56	game -> rest : hh = getHappyHour()
57	game -> rest : menu = getMenu()
58	game -> rest : waiter = getWaiter()

2 3/chat-01.md
Σ 13244
What did I learn?

I gained a better understanding of how OOP principles can be applied to accommodate
real life changing software requirements. I learned inheritance, polymorphism and
abstraction support the Open/Closed Principle by allowing new functionality to be
introduced without extensively modifying existing classes. I also improved my ability to
construct UML class diagrams and represent relationships between classes including
inheritance and associations. The project helped me see how they contribute to designing
a real system outside of only theory in lectures.

How did I apply it?

My main contribution was designing parts of Stage 2 and 3 UML class diagrams and
explaining how I implemented the SOLID principles.

For Stage 2, I focused on introducing VIPCustomer and CriticCustomer as subclasses of
the abstract Customer class. This allowed different customer types to have specialized
behaviors such as waiting satisfaction penalties, discounts and ordering preferences while
following the same general restaurant workflow.

For Stage 3, I focused on extending the menu system through ComboMeal which inherits
from MenuItem and contains two existing menu items. This design allows combo meals to
be treated as menu items withing the existing ordering structure.

These designs help me apple lecture content and understand the importance of designing
classes that can accommodate new requirements.

What challenged me?

One of the biggest challenges was determining how to incorporate the new requirements
into the existing class structure without creating unnecessary dependencies or modifying
too many classes. I initially found it difficult to decide whether new features should be
modelled as subclasses or handled by modifying existing classes.

Another challenge was constructing and presenting the UML diagrams. As more classes,
methods and relationships were introduced, the diagrams became increasingly
complicated and difficult to read. I also had limited experience using Google Colab and
Mermaid for UML diagrams, which made generating, formatting and exporting the
diagrams challenging.
How did I solve it?

To address the design challenges, I revisited the concepts of inheritance, polymorphism
and SOLID principles, particularly OCP and LSP. I examined how customer-specific
behaviors could be encapsulated within subclasses rather than implemented using
repeated conditional checks in the controller.

Similarly, for the Stage 3 menu extension, I used the existing MenuItem abstraction to
represent ComboMeal, allowing the design to reuse the ordering structure.

For the diagram-related difficulties, I experimented with different rendering and export
methods in Google Colab. After encountering issues with diagram scaling and image
generation, I exported the diagrams as PNG files instead of relying on screenshots. I also
organised the diagrams and design documentation for inclusion in the group's GitHub
repository and final report.

This experience taught me that designing extensible software requires more than applying
inheritance mechanically. It involves understanding each class's responsibility,
considering the impact of new requirements on existing components, and making design
decisions that maintain clarity and flexibility.

182	
183	  1. Add the required sequence diagram. Use one Stage 3 scenario in which a
184	  manager choice changes a customer's outcome, for example the Waiter serves one
185	  customer instead of taking another's order, so the second customer abandons.
186	  - Lifelines: Manager, RestaurantCLI, GameController, Restaurant,
187	    ArrivalSchedule, Host, Waiter, Chef, Cashier, HappyHour, VIPCustomer, Order,
188	    Table.
189	  - Order of events, labelled by turn:
190	    - arrival and order, with the Happy Hour activation and the VIP price lock
191	      ($15 → $13.50 → $10.80);
192	    - Chef progress, then READY;
193	    - Waiter serves on a later turn, with a guard that the order was already
194	      READY;
195	    - eating, then READY_TO_PAY;
196	    - Cashier pays on a later turn, then recordPayment;
197	    - the result check after turn 18.
198	  - Notation:
199	    - loop over turns 1..18;
200	    - alt for an invalid choice (error shown, re-prompt, no task used);
201	    - alt for abandonment at 0 satisfaction;
202	    - opt for Happy Hour;
203	    - alt for victory or defeat.
204	  - Briefing extras: state the scenario's stage, starting conditions and
205	    outcome.
206	
207	  2. Class diagram fixes:
208	  - Missing PAID: OrderStatus has no PAID and Order has no markPaid(), but the
209	    spec says payment marks the order PAID. Fix the diagram or the code, and add
210	    notes or a small state diagram for legal Order and Customer transitions.
211	  - Missing annotations: the assignment requires notes on encapsulation,
212	    polymorphism and composition, and there are none. Add a legend for the
213	    yellow, green and purple colouring, and a note documenting the Critic's
214	    rule.
215	  - Inconsistent arrows: Waiter → Table and Waiter → HappyHour are solid while
216	    Host → Table is dashed. ArrivalSchedule → Customer "creates" should be a
217	    dashed «create» dependency. The enum links are unlabelled. The RestaurantCLI
218	    ↔ GameController link is undirected, though each holds a reference to the
219	    other.
220	  - Composition notation: staff "employs" lines have no diamond, unlike Table,
221	    Menu and HappyHour.
222	  - Menu multiplicity: Stage 3 shows 5 on Menu → MenuItem (Stage 2 shows 3).
223	    Check it against the code, or use 3..*.
224	  - Combo pricing: show that ComboMeal sets its own 90% price and summed prep
225	    units, or add a note.
226	  - Missing dependencies: Waiter → Menu, Customer → Menu, Cashier → Restaurant
227	    and RestaurantCLI → Restaurant.
228	  - Queue: show the waiting queue as its own ordered association.
229	  - Readability: re-lay out the diagram to fit a landscape page. Printed in the
230	    PDF it will be unreadable at the current size.
231	  - Smaller points: the Stage 1 diagram has a typo (tabled for tableId) and is a
232	    low-res JPG in a different style from Stages 2 and 3. Also check whether
233	    #satisfaction needs to be protected.
234	
235	  3. Prepare the design discussion required by the public project description.
236	  - Waiter.takeOrder signature churn: it changes at every stage (+Menu,
237	    +currentTurn, then +HappyHour).
238	  - No pricing-policy abstraction: HappyHour is threaded through Restaurant,
239	    Waiter, Customer and GameController as a concrete class. Either introduce a
240	    pricing-policy abstraction, or justify in the report why it isn't needed.
241	    Don't add an interface just to tick a box.
242	  - Duplicated seating: Host.seatCustomer and Waiter.seatCustomer duplicate each
243	    other.
244	  - Change explanations: explain which Stage 2→3 changes to existing classes
245	    were necessary and why.
246	
247	  Before every tag, check each diagram against the code it describes, and commit
248	  the diagram sources (Mermaid or Visual Paradigm) as well as the images.
249	
250	  Adding the sequence diagram and addressing the issues above would improve
251	  alignment with the public project description. Final code alignment still
252	  needs checking against the runnable stage tags. I can draft the sequence
253	  diagram in Mermaid if you want.

## 0139 Assistant — 2026-11-02T15:29:07.176Z

Source record: 377; UUID: f2621ecb-49f8-4afe-8868-a51650233280.

**Verdict:** the diagrams are strong, but I wouldn't give them full marks yet. I estimate about 38–42 out of 45 for class plus sequence. The largest uncertainty is code alignment, which I couldn't check. I rendered all four PDFs and read the sources.

**What I couldn't verify**
- The repo has no Java source and no `stage-*` tags. `git ls-remote` shows only `main`.
- [src/README.md](docs/diagrams/src/README.md) says the diagrams "were updated without access to" the code.
- That puts the rubric's "matches the Stage 3 code" lines at risk: 4 class marks, 2 sequence marks, and part of the 6 for attributes and operations.

**Estimate by rubric line**

| Line | Est. | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Class: coverage (8) | 8 | All required elements are present, and the queue is shown as `{ordered, FIFO}`. |
| Class: relationships (7) | 5–6 | Multiplicity error and unreadable spots, listed below. |
| Class: attributes and encapsulation (6) | 5–6 | Order and Customer transition notes are good. |
| Class: clarity and alignment (4) | 2–3 | Alignment is unverified and the layout is crowded. |
| Sequence: scenario (5) | 4–5 | The Host is not drawn, and there is only one customer. |
| Sequence: food and payment flow (8) | 7–8 | Every required step is in the right order. |
| Sequence: OO messages and notation (5) | 4–5 | Guards and fragments are good. |
| Sequence: clarity (2) | 1–2 | A few inconsistencies, listed below. |

**Strengths**
- I checked every sequence message against the Stage 3 class diagram. All of them exist there.
- The turn rules hold up: ready on turn 2, served on 3, ready to pay on 4, paid on 5. The VIP bill goes $15.00 → $13.50 → $10.80, and satisfaction ends at 95 (90 if Happy Hour is declined).
- Polymorphism, price locking and the Stage 3 Happy Hour behaviour are all visible in the sequence.
- The notes, colour key and change lists meet the "annotate encapsulation, polymorphism, composition" requirement.

**To reach full marks, in priority order**
1. **Check the diagrams against the tagged code.** Work through the checklist in [src/README.md](docs/diagrams/src/README.md). Run the base setting once and confirm the sequence's numbers: turn-1 VIP, Burger via `customerId % 5`, and how `serveOrder` rejects an invalid serve.
2. **Fix the `occupant` multiplicity.** `Customer "0..1" -- "1" Table` is wrong because a queued customer has no table ([stage-3-class.puml:297](docs/diagrams/src/stage-3-class.puml#L297), and the same line in Stages 1 and 2). It should be a directed `Table "1" --> "0..1" Customer`, since Table holds the reference.
3. **Untangle the Customer corner.** The `customers`, `waitingQueue` and `occupant` multiplicity labels (`0..*`, `0..*`, `0..1`) stack on one point in all three class diagrams and can't be read.
4. **Separate the ComboMeal lines.** [stage-3-class.puml:293-294](docs/diagrams/src/stage-3-class.puml#L293-L294) draws the inheritance and the aggregation as two near-identical lines, with `2` and `0..*` colliding with the triangle and diamond. The rubric singles out the combo, so make both relationships obvious.
5. **Make the sequence match the base setting.** The note says base setting, but the Host prompt is missing and the turn-4 arrival is skipped. Add a one-line `readHostChoice()` → WAIT and say the other arrivals are omitted.
6. **Strengthen the manager decision (optional).** Everything currently runs through one customer, so no staff task is contested and the diagram never shows how the manager picks which customer. A second customer would show a real priority choice.
7. **Correct the "Declined" note.** `isAvailable()` stays true after a decline, so the manager can still activate on turn 2. The note should say never activated.
8. **Housekeeping.**
   - The README says `stage-1-class.uml` is "kept as the archive", but git shows it deleted and uncommitted. Restore it or fix the README.
   - `README.md` and `render.mjs` are untracked.
   - Sequence page 3 is more than half blank.
   - The diagrams are 1,500–1,850 pt wide, so they will be tiny on an A4 page in the report. Give them landscape or A3 pages.

**Not diagram errors, but be ready to justify them in the report**
- Stage 1 → 2 removes `canFinishEating`, `canPay` and `findOldestEligiblePayer`, moving the turn rules into `GameController`.
- Stage 1 → 2 changes five existing classes.
- `Host` and `Waiter` both define `seatCustomer(Customer, Table)`.

These touch the rubric's change-analysis and "no god class" lines.

I removed the screenshots I made in the repo root, so the working tree is as I found it.

**Why I stopped:** Rendering needs a PlantUML jar, and there isn't one on this machine. I downloaded the 1.2025.4 jar (the version your README names) into my scratchpad. The permission classifier then blocked my script that would run it, so I didn't try to get around that. Without rendering I can't check the layout fixes for issues 3 and 4. I also can't confirm the new sequence content compiles, or regenerate the PDFs.

**Done so far (uncommitted):**
- **Issue 2:** the `occupant` link is now `Table "1" -down-> "0..1" Customer` in all three class sources.
- **Issue 7:** the sequence notes now say Happy Hour can still be switched on in turn 2, with the same outcome here, and "never activated" gives $13.50 and satisfaction 90.
- **Issue 8, partly:** I restored `stage-1-class.uml` from git, so the README's "archive" claim is true again. `README.md` and `render.mjs` are still untracked and will go in the commit.

**Still to do:**
- **Issue 3:** untangle the stacked multiplicity labels at the Customer corner.
- **Issue 4:** separate the ComboMeal inheritance and aggregation lines.
- **Issue 5:** add `readHostChoice()` and the turn-4 arrival to the sequence.
- **Issue 6:** I'd only add customer 2's arrival at turn 4, with the Host seating them. A real contested choice doesn't arise in the base schedule, because arrivals are three turns apart. I'd say so rather than invent a scenario.
- **Issue 8, rest:** per-page PDF sizing so sequence page 3 isn't half blank, plus README guidance on page size for the report.
- Regenerate the four PDFs and commit.

The Host prompt position and customer 2's type are assumptions, since I can't see the code. I'd record them in the README next to the existing "check against the code" list.

**Decision for you:** can I run the downloaded jar (`...\scratchpad\plantuml\plantuml-1.2025.4.jar`)? 1	@startuml
2	' ======================================================================
3	' SC2002 Restaurant Rush - Stage 3 sequence diagram (source of truth)
4	' Scenario: a VIP orders a Burger while the manager has Happy Hour on.
5	' Turns 1-5 are unrolled so that "later turn" rules are visible:
6	'   T1 arrive/seat -> T2 order + Chef READY -> T3 serve -> T4 READY_TO_PAY -> T5 pay
7	' Render: see docs/diagrams/src/README.md
8	' ======================================================================

10	mainframe **sd** VipOrdersBurgerDuringHappyHour
11	
12	skinparam style strictuml
13	skinparam defaultFontName SansSerif
14	skinparam defaultFontSize 12
15	skinparam shadowing false
16	skinparam ParticipantPadding 6
17	skinparam BoxPadding 4
18	skinparam sequenceMessageAlign center
19	skinparam responseMessageBelowArrow true
20	skinparam sequenceGroupHeaderFontStyle bold

22	skinparam sequenceGroupBodyBackgroundColor transparent
23	skinparam sequenceDividerBackgroundColor #E3EAF4
24	skinparam sequenceDividerBorderColor #7C93B8
25	skinparam sequenceDividerFontStyle bold
26	skinparam noteBackgroundColor #FFF4CF
27	skinparam noteBorderColor #B08A2E
28	skinparam noteFontSize 11
29	skinparam sequenceLifeLineBorderColor #999999
30	skinparam ArrowColor #222222
31	
32	actor Manager as M order 10
33	boundary "cli : RestaurantCLI" as cli order 20
34	control "game : GameController" as game order 30
35	entity "schedule : ArrivalSchedule" as sched order 40
36	entity "restaurant : Restaurant" as rest order 50
37	entity "hh : HappyHour" as hh order 60
38	entity "t1 : Table" as t1 order 70
39	entity "waiter : Waiter" as waiter order 90
40	entity "chef : Chef" as chef order 110
41	entity "cashier : Cashier" as cashier order 120
42	' vip (order 80) and order (order 100) are declared where they are created
43	
44	note over M, rest
45	  <b>Stage 3 | base setting (18 turns, 2 tables)</b>
46	  <b>Start:</b> turn 1, restaurant empty, 2 free tables. A VIP arrives
47	  (satisfaction 100). Burger = $15.00, 1 preparation unit.
48	  <b>Manager choice:</b> turn on Happy Hour at turn 1 or 2 (or never).
49	  <b>Outcome:</b> the VIP is PAID on turn 5, bill locked at <b>$10.80</b>,
50	  satisfaction <b>95</b>. If never activated: bill $13.50, satisfaction 90.
51	  <b>Not drawn:</b> the Host option is left at WAIT (the Waiter seats the VIP).
52	end note
53	
54	M -> cli : start game
55	cli -> game : startGame()
56	game -> rest : hh = getHappyHour()
57	game -> rest : menu = getMenu()
58	game -> rest : waiter = getWaiter()
59	game -> rest : chef = getChef()
60	game -> rest : cashier = getCashier()

## 0308 Assistant — 2026-11-02T15:47:09.785Z

I've fixed issues 2–8 and committed everything as `58673b8`. The four PDFs were regenerated with the jar you approved, and I checked the rendered output.

Issue 1, checking the diagrams against the code, is still open. I can't see the code, so I added what I assumed to the "check against the code" list in the README.

- **Occupant link (2):** `occupant` is now a directed link from `Table`, with `[0..1]` at the customer end, in all three class diagrams.
- **Customer corner (3):** the multiplicities for `customers`, `waitingQueue`, `occupant` and `order` now sit in the link labels, so nothing overlaps at the `Customer` corner. This is a notation trade-off: those ends no longer carry a conventional end-of-line multiplicity.
- **ComboMeal (4):** the inheritance and aggregation lines still join the same two boxes and run close together. The aggregation is now orange and labelled `components [2]`, so you can tell them apart. The COMBO note explains this.
- **Sequence diagram (5):** I added the `host : Host` lifeline and `readHostChoice()` in turn 1, with Host WAIT notes for turns 2 and 3. Turn 4 now shows customer 2's arrival and the Host seating them. There is a note that the manager also picks which customer goes first when several qualify.
- **Contested choice (6):** I did not add one. The base schedule spaces arrivals three turns apart, so no real contention arises in this scenario. The diagram shows the Host seating customer 2 independently of the Waiter, and the Happy Hour decision remains the choice that changes the outcome.
- **"Declined" note (7):** it now says Happy Hour can still be switched on in turn 2 with the same outcome, and that never activating gives $13.50 and satisfaction 90.
- **Housekeeping (8):**
  - I restored `stage-1-class.uml`, so the README's "archive" claim is true again.
  - `render.mjs` now gives each PDF page its own height, so sequence page 3 no longer has blank space.
  - The README now documents the drawing conventions and has sizing advice for the report. The PDFs are large, and text drops to about 4 pt on A4 landscape, so use A3 or full pages.

Things I assumed that are unverified against the code:
- The Host step is drawn before `coordinateStaffActions()`.
- `readHostChoice()` returns SEAT or WAIT.
- Customer 2's type is left open.

The TA rubric PDF is still untracked and not in the commit.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - **Request 1:** Inspect all diagrams under `docs/diagrams` against the project briefing (`docs/SC2002_Group_Project_Briefing.pdf`), the assignment brief (`docs/SC2002_Restaurant_Rush_Group_Assignment.docx`) and the TA marking rubric (`SC2002 Restaurant Rush TA Marking Rubric.pdf`, repo root, untracked). Give a final verdict and say what to improve for full marks, or whether it already deserves full marks.
   - **Request 2:** "Fix all issues besides issue 1, then commit". Issue 1 is verifying code alignment, which was skipped because the repo has no Java source or stage tags.
   - **Request 3:** "You are approved to use the jar, proceed". This authorised running the PlantUML jar I had downloaded after the permission classifier blocked it.
   - **Status:** Everything requested is done and committed as `58673b8`. The only remaining step is the final reply to the user summarising the outcome.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Course project: SC2002 Restaurant Rush, a Java CLI turn-based restaurant game in 3 stages (tags stage-1/2/3), with 3 class diagrams plus 1 sequence diagram required.
   - Rubric weights: class diagram 25 (coverage 8, relationships/notation 7, attributes/ops/encapsulation 6, clarity/code alignment 4). Sequence diagram 20 (scenario 5, food/payment flow 8, OO messages/notation 5, clarity 2).
   - Game rules relevant to the diagrams:
     - 18 turns, 2 tables, one Waiter, Chef and Cashier. Host seats one queued customer per turn (Stage 3).
     - Customer loss per waiting turn: Regular 8, VIP 5, Critic 12. VIP gets 10% off.
     - Combo price is 90% of the sum, rounded. Happy Hour gives 20% off for 2 turns, once per game, +15 satisfaction recovery per active turn, and the VIP discount applies first.
     - Customers served on turn N become READY_TO_PAY on N+1 and pay on N+2 at the earliest.
     - Victory: ≥4 paid, revenue ≥ $45, average satisfaction ≥ 60.
   - PlantUML 1.2025.4 with the Smetana layout (`!pragma layout smetana`, no Graphviz), rendered to SVG then to vector PDF through headless Chrome (`render.mjs`).
   - Smetana limitations:
     - Two edges between the same pair of nodes run nearly parallel.
     - Labelled same-rank links can't be routed.
     - `[norank]` had no visible effect.
     - End multiplicity labels collide where several edges converge on one corner.
   - PlantUML arrow style syntax: style goes before direction, e.g. `o-[#B5541C,bold]up-`; hidden links are `-[hidden]right-`.
   - Chrome named `@page` rules give per-page PDF sizes.
   - Environment quirks:
     - Windows with a Git Bash tool; Python is not installed.
     - `pdftotext` is available; `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` are not.
     - Java 25 and Chrome are present.
     - A heredoc through the Bash tool stripped backslashes, so JS files containing `\\` were created with the Write tool.
     - The Playwright MCP can only save screenshots under the repo root.

34	Cashier × 1 (one payment per turn)
35	Arrivals
36	One customer at the start of turns 1, 4, 7, 10, 13 and 16 (six in total)
37	Objective
38	At the end of turn 18: at least 4 paid customers AND revenue at least $45 AND average satisfaction of paid customers at least 60
39	
40	Menu item
41	Price
42	Preparation units
43	Salad
44	$9.00
45	1
46	Burger
47	$15.00
48	1
49	Pasta
50	$18.00
51	2
52	
53	2.2  General rules
54	Customers choose their own food. The manager never chooses it. Use a documented deterministic rule or seeded randomness, and show the choice once the order is placed.
55	Keep rules out of the UI. The UI only collects the manager’s decisions and displays the state. Game rules belong in domain and control objects.
56	Money. If fractional prices arise, store amounts as integer cents. Display dollars with two decimals.
57	Offline. All mandatory features must work without network access.
58	3  How a turn works
59	3.1  Start-of-turn display
60	At the start of every turn, show: the turn number, revenue, average satisfaction of paid customers, the waiting queue, occupied tables, each customer’s satisfaction, order status, and the staff tasks available.
61	3.2  Turn steps
62	Each turn then runs these six steps in order.
63	Step
64	What happens
65	1  Arrivals
66	The scheduled customer joins the waiting queue with satisfaction 100.
67	2  Manager choices
68	Waiter: choose at most one task, or Wait: seat one queued customer at a free table, take one seated customer’s order, or serve one READY order.
69	Chef: choose at most one order to advance by one preparation unit, or Wait. The Chef may work on an order the Waiter took this turn.
70	3  Kitchen and serving
71	The Waiter acts before the Chef. When an order’s last preparation unit is done, it becomes READY. A dish can be served only if it was already READY when the Waiter acts, so a dish finished this turn is served next turn at the earliest. Serving sets it to SERVED.
72	4  Eating and payment
73	A customer served on an earlier turn finishes one full turn of eating and becomes READY_TO_PAY. The Cashier automatically handles at most one READY_TO_PAY customer per turn, oldest first. A customer who became READY_TO_PAY this turn pays next turn at the earliest. Payment adds the bill to revenue, marks the customer and order PAID, and frees the table.
74	5  Waiting and abandonment
75	At turn end, every waiting or seated customer who has not been served loses satisfaction (Regular: 8 points, minimum 0). A customer who reaches 0 leaves without paying: cancel any unfinished order and free the table. Customers who are served, eating or ready to pay lose nothing.
76	6  Result
77	The game always runs all 18 turns; there is no early ending. After turn 18: Victory if all three targets in Section 2.1 are met, otherwise Defeat.
78	
79	3.3  Example: one customer from arrival to payment
80	This example follows one Regular customer who orders Pasta (2 preparation units) and gets the manager’s full attention.
81	Turn
82	What happens
83	Satisfaction at turn end
84	1
85	Arrives (100). Waiter seats the customer.
86	92 (seated, not served)
87	2
88	Waiter takes the Pasta order. Chef does unit 1 of 2.
89	84
90	3
91	Chef does unit 2 of 2: order is READY. The Waiter cannot serve it this turn.
92	76
93	4
94	Waiter serves: SERVED.
95	76 (no further loss)
96	5
97	Customer finishes eating: READY_TO_PAY.
98	76
99	6
100	Cashier collects $18.00: PAID, table freed. Satisfaction 76 is recorded.
101	—
102	
103	3.4  Rules that always apply
104	A Waiter completes at most one task per turn.
105	A table stays occupied until its customer pays or leaves.
106	A customer cannot eat before being served. The Cashier cannot charge before READY_TO_PAY.
107	An invalid choice must explain why it failed and let the manager choose again, without using up the staff task.
108	Revenue never includes unpaid or abandoned orders.
109	The customer who arrives on turn 16 cannot finish paying within 18 turns, so at most five customers can pay. This is intended, not a bug.
110	3.5  End-of-turn results
111	Show the served count, unhappy departures, revenue, average satisfaction of paid customers, and turns used. The average uses the satisfaction recorded when each customer pays. If no one has paid yet, show N/A.
112	4  The three stages
113	Each stage is a separate runnable version tagged in GitHub, and each builds on the one before.
114	Stage
115	Adds
116	Git tag
117	1  Basic operation
118	Regular customers, menu, Waiter, Chef, Cashier, full turn sequence, both endings
119	stage-1
120	2  Customer behaviour
121	VIP and Critic customers
122	stage-2
123	3  New role and menu policy
124	Host, ComboMeal, Happy Hour
125	stage-3
126	
127	4.1  Stage 1: Basic restaurant operation
128	Build a complete playable game containing:
129	regular customers and the three menu items;
130	Waiter, Chef, Cashier, Table, Order and the waiting queue;
131	the full turn sequence, including satisfaction, abandonment, payment, victory and defeat.
132	The player sets seating, ordering and serving priority through the Waiter, and kitchen priority through the Chef.
133	Stage 1 must: compile and run on its own at tag stage-1; contain no Stage 2 or Stage 3 features; include one reproducible victory run and one reproducible defeat run in your test notes.
134	4.2  Stage 2: Customer behaviour extension
135	Add VIPCustomer and CriticCustomer alongside regular customers.
136	Customer
137	Satisfaction Loss per waiting turn
138	Bill
139	Special behaviour
140	Regular
141	8
142	Full price
143	—
144	VIP
145	5
146	10% off
147	—
148	Critic
149	12
150	Full price
151	A distinct order preference or reaction to service that you define and document
152	
153	A reproducible base-setting run must include at least one VIP and one Critic. You may replace two of the regular arrivals; there must still be six arrivals.
154	All customer types follow the same order and service flow.
155	Different behaviour must be dispatched through the customer objects, not through a chain of type checks in the turn controller.
156	Stage 2 is independently playable and uses the same win and lose criteria.
157	4.3  Stage 3: New role and menu policy
158	Host. The Host seats at most one queued customer per turn, in addition to the Waiter’s task. The Waiter can still seat customers.
159	ComboMeal. A combo is made of two existing menu items.
160	Preparation units = the sum of its two components.
161	Price = 90% of the components’ combined price, rounded to the nearest cent. Example: Salad + Pasta = ($9 + $18) × 0.9 = $24.30, 3 units.
162	A customer chooses a combo according to a rule you document.
163	Happy Hour. A pricing policy the manager may switch on.
164	Can be activated once per game, at the start of a turn. It lasts for that turn and the next. Display its remaining duration.
165	Orders taken while it is active get 20% off their bill.
166	During each active turn, customers still waiting for a table or food recover 15 satisfaction at turn start (maximum 100).
167	Apply the VIP discount first, then Happy Hour. The price is locked when the order is taken. Example: a VIP orders a Burger during Happy Hour: $15 × 0.9 × 0.8 = $10.80.
168	It does not change orders already placed, and does not affect customers who have left.
169	Stage 3 is independently playable.
170	4.4  Design freedom and changes to earlier code
171	You choose class names and design patterns. Do not create interfaces just to satisfy a checklist.
172	If a later stage needs you to modify an earlier class, explain why. Small registration or setup changes are expected.
173	If you broadly rewrite stable game rules, explain why and show that earlier behaviour still works.
174	Extra features earn credit only when they show sound design and do not replace required rules.
175	5  Design evidence
176	Include these in your PDF report and repository.

181	Show the principal entity classes, necessary control and boundary classes, relevant enums, key attributes and operations with visibility, inheritance, and associations with multiplicities where relevant.
182	Annotate meaningful examples of encapsulation, polymorphism and composition.
183	Sequence diagram
184	One complete scenario in which a manager choice changes an outcome.
185	Include: customer arrival and order; Waiter and Chef assignments; READY then SERVED; eating; payment; the result check; one Stage 2 or 3 behaviour (e.g. VIP pricing or Happy Hour).
186	Show messages among the CLI, controller, staff and domain objects, with relevant guards and loops.
187	Design discussion
188	A table of key abstractions and their responsibilities.
189	SRP, OCP, LSP, ISP and DIP, only where genuinely reflected in your code.
190	Trade-offs, and one alternative design you considered.
191	How the player’s choices change a result.
192	GitHub evidence
193	Tags stage-1, stage-2 and stage-3 on runnable commits.
194	In the README, for each transition (1 → 2 and 2 → 3): new files; existing files changed or deleted, with reasons; the relevant commit or compare link.
195	The source code itself, not only the README, must show the real stages.

173	' ---------- layout skeleton (hidden links keep the rows tidy) ----------
174	Table -[hidden]right- Waiter
175	Waiter -[hidden]right- Chef
176	Chef -[hidden]right- Cashier
177	Cashier -[hidden]right- MenuItem
178	Customer -[hidden]right- Order
179	Order -[hidden]right- CustomerStatus
180	CustomerStatus -[hidden]right- OrderStatus
181	
182	' ---------- control and boundary ----------
183	RestaurantCLI "cli 1" <-down-> "controller 1" GameController
184	GameController "1" -down-> "restaurant 1" Restaurant
185	RestaurantCLI .down.> Restaurant : displays
186	
187	' ---------- composition (Restaurant owns these) ----------
188	Restaurant "1" *-down- "2" Table : tables
189	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Waiter : waiter
190	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Chef : chef
191	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Cashier : cashier
192	
193	' ---------- aggregation (customers arrive and leave; menu items are shared) ----------
194	' Multiplicities at the Customer end sit in the labels: three links meet at Customer's corner and end labels would overlap there.
195	Restaurant "1" o-down- Customer : customers [0..*]
196	Restaurant "1" o-down- Customer : waitingQueue [0..*]\n{ordered, FIFO}
197	Restaurant "1" o-down- "3" MenuItem : menuItems
198	
199	' ---------- domain associations ----------
200	Table "1" -down-> Customer : occupant [0..1]
201	Customer "1" -right-> Order : order [0..1]
202	Order "0..*" -right-> "1" MenuItem
203	Customer -down-> CustomerStatus : status
204	Order -down-> OrderStatus : status
205	
206	' ---------- dependencies ----------
207	Waiter .left.> Table
208	Waiter .down.> Customer : seats / serves
209	Waiter .down.> Order : creates
210	Waiter .down.> MenuItem : reads
211	Chef .down.> Order : advances
212	Cashier .down.> Customer : charges
213	Cashier .up.> Restaurant : recordPayment,\nreleaseTableOf
214	
215	' ---------- annotations ----------
216	note top of Restaurant
217	  <b>COMPOSITION</b>
218	  Restaurant creates and owns its Tables and staff;
219	  they exist only as part of one Restaurant.
220	  Customers are aggregated: they arrive and leave.
221	  The waitingQueue and customers links both point
222	  at Customer. Stage 1 has only Regular customers.
223	end note
224	
225	note bottom of Order
226	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
227	  status is private and changes only through
228	  advancePreparation(), markServed(), markPaid(),
229	  cancel(); illegal transitions are rejected.
230	  Legal: PLACED → PREPARING → READY → SERVED → PAID
231	  (PLACED → READY directly for a 1-unit dish);
232	  PLACED / PREPARING / READY → CANCELLED if the
233	  customer leaves. The price is read from the item.
234	end note
235	
236	note bottom of CustomerStatus
237	  <b>CUSTOMER LIFECYCLE</b>
238	  WAITING → SEATED → ORDERED → SERVED →
239	  READY_TO_PAY → PAID; WAITING / SEATED / ORDERED
240	  → ABANDONED when satisfaction reaches 0.
241	  Served, eating and READY_TO_PAY customers
242	  lose no satisfaction.
243	end note
244	
245	note left of Customer
246	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
247	  satisfaction and status are private and change only
248	  through reduceSatisfaction() and the mark...() methods.
249	  <b>TURN GUARDS</b>
250	  canFinishEating(turn): one full turn after serving.
251	  canPay(turn): only after the READY_TO_PAY turn.
252	  Regular customers lose 8 per waiting turn.
253	end note
254	
255	note right of Waiter
256	  <b>STAFF ROLES</b>
257	  One class per role: the Waiter seats, takes orders
258	  and serves; the Chef prepares; the Cashier takes
259	  payment. Each does at most one task per turn.
260	end note
261	@enduml
262

226	  +Order(item: MenuItem, billCents: int, turnTaken: int)
227	  +advancePreparation(): void
228	  +isReady(): boolean
229	  +markServed(): void
230	  +markPaid(): void
231	  +cancel(): void
232	  +getStatus(): OrderStatus
233	  +getBillCents(): int
234	  +getTurnTaken(): int
235	}
236	
237	enum CustomerStatus #EAF1F7 {
238	  WAITING
239	  SEATED
240	  ORDERED
241	  SERVED
242	  READY_TO_PAY
243	  PAID
244	  ABANDONED
245	}
246	
247	enum OrderStatus #EAF1F7 {
248	  PLACED
249	  PREPARING
250	  READY
251	  SERVED
252	  PAID
253	  CANCELLED
254	}
255	
256	' ---------- layout skeleton (hidden links keep the rows tidy) ----------
257	' Smetana cannot route labelled same-rank links, so sideways links carry no mid-link label.
258	Restaurant -[hidden]right- GameController
259	GameController -[hidden]right- ArrivalSchedule
260	Host -[hidden]right- Table
261	Table -[hidden]right- Waiter
262	Waiter -[hidden]right- Chef
263	Chef -[hidden]right- Cashier
264	Cashier -[hidden]right- Menu
265	Customer -[hidden]right- Order
266	Order -[hidden]right- MenuItem
267	RegularCustomer -[hidden]right- VIPCustomer
268	VIPCustomer -[hidden]right- CriticCustomer
269	CriticCustomer -[hidden]right- CustomerStatus
270	
271	' ---------- control and boundary ----------
272	RestaurantCLI "cli 1" <-down-> "controller 1" GameController
273	GameController "1" -right-> "schedule 1" ArrivalSchedule
274	GameController "1" -left-> "restaurant 1" Restaurant
275	RestaurantCLI .down.> Restaurant : displays
276	ArrivalSchedule .down.> Customer : <<create>>\nRegular, VIP, Critic
277	
278	' ---------- composition (Restaurant owns these) ----------
279	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" HappyHour : happyHour
280	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Host : host
281	Restaurant "1" *-down- "2" Table : tables
282	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Waiter : waiter
283	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Chef : chef
284	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Cashier : cashier
285	Restaurant "1" *-down- "1" Menu : menu
286	
287	' ---------- aggregation (customers arrive and leave) ----------
288	' Multiplicities at the Customer end sit in the labels: three links meet at Customer's corner and end labels would overlap there.
289	Restaurant "1" o-down- Customer : customers [0..*]
290	Restaurant "1" o-down- Customer : waitingQueue [0..*]\n{ordered, FIFO}
291	
292	' ---------- menu ----------
293	Menu "1" o-down- "3..*" MenuItem : items
294	MenuItem <|-down- ComboMeal
295	' Inheritance and aggregation join the same two boxes, so the aggregation is coloured and its multiplicity sits in the label
296	ComboMeal o-[#B5541C,bold]up- MenuItem : components [2]
297	
298	' ---------- domain associations ----------
299	Table "1" -down-> Customer : occupant [0..1]
300	Customer "1" -right-> Order : order [0..1]
301	Order "0..*" -right-> "1" MenuItem
302	Customer -down-> CustomerStatus : status
303	Order -down-> OrderStatus : status
304	Customer <|-down- RegularCustomer
305	Customer <|-down- VIPCustomer
306	Customer <|-down- CriticCustomer
307	
308	' ---------- dependencies ----------
309	GameController .[#777777].> Waiter
310	GameController .[#777777].> Host
311	GameController .[#777777].> Chef
312	GameController .[#777777].> Cashier
313	GameController .[#777777].> HappyHour
314	GameController .[#777777].> Customer
315	Host .right.> Table
316	Waiter .left.> Table
317	Host .down.> Customer : seats
318	Waiter .down.> Customer : seats / serves
319	Waiter .down.> Order : <<create>>
320	Waiter .down.> Menu : reads
321	Waiter .up.> HappyHour : applyDiscount
322	Chef .down.> Order : advances
323	Cashier .down.> Customer : charges
324	Cashier .down.> Order : reads bill
325	Cashier .up.> Restaurant : recordPayment,\nreleaseTableOf
326	Customer .up.> Menu : chooses from
327	
328	' ---------- annotations ----------
329	note top of Restaurant
330	  <b>COMPOSITION</b>
331	  Restaurant creates and owns its Tables, Menu,
332	  HappyHour and staff; they exist only as part of
333	  one Restaurant. Customers are aggregated:
334	  they arrive and leave. The waitingQueue and
335	  customers links both point at Customer.
336	end note
337	
338	note bottom of Order
339	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
340	  status is private and changes only through
341	  advancePreparation(), markServed(), markPaid(),
342	  cancel(); illegal transitions are rejected.
343	  Legal: PLACED → PREPARING → READY → SERVED → PAID
344	  (PLACED → READY directly for a 1-unit dish);
345	  PLACED / PREPARING / READY → CANCELLED if the
346	  customer leaves.
347	  billCents is set once in the constructor with no
348	  setter: the price is locked when ordered.
349	end note
350	
351	note bottom of CustomerStatus
352	  <b>CUSTOMER LIFECYCLE</b>
353	  WAITING → SEATED → ORDERED → SERVED →
354	  READY_TO_PAY → PAID; WAITING / SEATED / ORDERED
355	  → ABANDONED when satisfaction reaches 0.
356	  Served, eating and READY_TO_PAY customers
357	  lose no satisfaction.
358	end note
359	
360	note left of Customer
361	  <b>POLYMORPHISM</b>
362	  getSatisfactionLoss(), calculateBillCents(),
363	  chooseMenuItem() and onServed() are overridden.
364	  GameController and Waiter call them on Customer
365	  and never check the type.
366	  Loss per waiting turn: Regular 8, VIP 5, Critic 12.
367	  <b>ENCAPSULATION</b>
368	  satisfaction is private; subclasses change it only
369	  through adjustSatisfaction() (clamped 0..100).
370	  <b>TURN GUARDS</b>
371	  Eats only if currentTurn > servedTurn;
372	  pays only if currentTurn > readyTurn.
373	end note
374	
375	note right of Waiter
376	  <b>BILLING</b>
377	  takeOrder(): bill = happyHour.applyDiscount(
378	    customer.calculateBillCents(price))
379	  VIP discount first, then Happy Hour; locked in
380	  the new Order. VIP Burger: 1500, 1350, 1080 cents.
381	end note
382	
383	note right of HappyHour
384	  <b>HAPPY HOUR</b>
385	  Once per game, at turn start; active for that turn
386	  and the next. 20% off orders taken; +15 satisfaction
387	  (max 100) for customers waiting for a table or food.
388	end note
389	
390	note right of CriticCustomer
391	  <b>CRITIC RULE</b>
392	  Loses 12 per waiting turn. Always orders the most
393	  expensive menu item (Salad + Pasta, $24.30, 3 units).
394	  Served more than 2 turns after ordering: loses an
395	  extra 10 (onServed).
396	end note
397	
398	note bottom of ComboMeal
399	  <b>COMBO</b>
400	  Hollow-triangle line: a ComboMeal is a MenuItem. Orange
401	  diamond line: it holds 2 MenuItem components (a MenuItem
402	  can be a component of 0..* combos).
403	  Stage 3 menu =Salad, Burger, Pasta + 2 ComboMeals
404	  (5 entries), registered once at game setup.
405	  Customer.chooseMenuItem(menu) picks from it.
406	  price = (a.price + b.price) x 90%, rounded:
407	  (sumCents x 90 + 50) / 100; units = a.units + b.units.
408	  Salad + Burger $21.60, 2 units; Salad + Pasta $24.30,
409	  3 units. Choice rule: customerId % menu.size(), so
410	  indexes 3 and 4 are the combos.
411	end note
412	
413	legend left
414	  <b>KEY</b>
415	  <back:#CCEBD2>  green  </back> new in Stage 3
416	  <back:#FAE8A6>  yellow  </back> changed since Stage 2
417	  <back:#DCEBF7>  blue  </back> unchanged
418	  <b>Changed since Stage 2</b> (nothing removed)
419	  Customer: + recoverSatisfaction(), isAwaitingService()
420	  Waiter: takeOrder(...) also takes HappyHour
421	  MenuItem: public constructor (ComboMeal calls super)
422	  Restaurant: + host, happyHour and their getters
423	  GameController, RestaurantCLI: Host and Happy Hour steps
424	end legend
425	@enduml
426

1	@startuml
2	' ======================================================================
3	' SC2002 Restaurant Rush - Stage 3 sequence diagram (source of truth)
4	' Scenario: a VIP orders a Burger while the manager has Happy Hour on.
5	' Turns 1-5 are unrolled so that "later turn" rules are visible:
6	'   T1 arrive/seat -> T2 order + Chef READY -> T3 serve -> T4 READY_TO_PAY -> T5 pay
7	' Turn 4 also has the base-setting arrival of customer 2, seated by the Host.
8	' Render: see docs/diagrams/src/README.md
9	' ======================================================================
10	title Restaurant Rush: Stage 3 sequence diagram (page 1 of 3)
11	mainframe **sd** VipOrdersBurgerDuringHappyHour
12

15	skinparam defaultFontSize 12
16	skinparam shadowing false
17	skinparam ParticipantPadding 6

20	skinparam responseMessageBelowArrow true

33	actor Manager as M order 10
34	boundary "cli : RestaurantCLI" as cli order 20
35	control "game : GameController" as game order 30
36	entity "schedule : ArrivalSchedule" as sched order 40
37	entity "restaurant : Restaurant" as rest order 50
